## Summary

Lenin’s 1916 pamphlet, presented by Socialism for All, argues that imperialism is capitalism’s highest and final stage, defined by the concentration of production into monopolies, the fusion of bank and industrial capital into finance capital, capital export, colonial partition of the world, and inter-imperialist war. It cites J. A. Hobson’s Imperialism and Rudolf Hilferding’s Finance Capital, criticizes Karl Kautsky, Otto Bauer, Ramsay MacDonald, Albert Thomas, Bernstein, Millerand, Heinemann, Gumpers, the Second International, Mensheviks, Socialist Revolutionaries, Scheidemann, Noske, and Wilsonism, while praising Bolshevik revolutionary defeatism and the Basel Manifesto’s revolutionary orientation. Key data include German industrial censuses showing large enterprises employing over 50 workers rising from 3 to 9 per 1,000 between 1882 and 1907 and employing 37% of workers; broad German industry where 0.9% of enterprises used 75.3% of steam horsepower and 77.2% of electricity; U.S. statistics where about 1.1% of enterprises in 1909 produced 43.8% of output; capital exports yielding 8–10 billion francs annually pre-war; and railway/colonial partition data for 1876/1914 and 1890/1913 as evidence that World War I was a predatory war for markets, colonies, and spheres of influence, with Brest-Litovsk and Versailles exposing pacifist illusions.

Treat the text as a research framework rather than a health guide; no direct health implications appear in the transcript. Read the full pamphlet plus its prefaces, Hobson, Hilferding, the Basel Manifesto, Lenin’s 1915 “The Defeat of One’s Own Government in the Imperialist War,” and critiques of Kautskyism/Ultra-imperialism; then test the claims against modern data on corporate concentration, vertical integration, bank–industry links, foreign direct investment, sovereign debt, defense-industrial supply chains, and union/political leadership. Decide whether current conflicts are best analyzed as imperialist competition, defensive national liberation, or mixed cases, and whether “labor aristocracy” explains reformist politics. Follow-up questions: Which indicators show monopoly/finance capital today? How do the U.S., EU, China, Russia, and Global South states fit the model? What evidence would falsify the claim that inter-imperialist war is inevitable under private property? How should activists distinguish anti-imperialism from great-power chauvinism?

[00:52:55] Chapter two banks and their new role.
[00:52:58] The principle and primary function of
[00:52:59] banks is to serve as middlemen in the
[00:53:01] making of payments. In so doing, they
[00:53:04] transform inactive money capital into
[00:53:06] active that is into capital yielding a
[00:53:08] profit. They collect all kinds of money
[00:53:10] revenues and place them at the disposal
[00:53:12] of the capitalist class. As banking
[00:53:14] develops and becomes concentrated in a
[00:53:17] small number of establishments, the
[00:53:18] banks grow from modest middlemen into
[00:53:20] powerful monopolies, having at their
[00:53:22] command almost the whole of the money
[00:53:24] capital of all the capitalists and small
[00:53:26] businessmen and also the larger part of
[00:53:28] the means of production and sources of
[00:53:30] raw materials in any one country and in
[00:53:32] a number of countries. This
[00:53:33] transformation of numerous modest
[00:53:35] middlemen into a handful of monopolists
[00:53:37] is one of the fundamental processes in

[02:40:37] Chapter 7. Imperialism as a special
[02:40:39] stage of capitalism. We must now try to
[02:40:42] sum up to draw together the threads of
[02:40:44] what has been said above on the subject
[02:40:45] of imperialism. Imperialism emerged as
[02:40:48] the development and direct continuation
[02:40:50] of the fundamental characteristics of
[02:40:52] capitalism in general. But capitalism
[02:40:54] only became capitalist imperialism at a
[02:40:57] definite and very high stage of its
[02:40:58] development. When certain of its
[02:41:00] fundamental characteristics began to
[02:41:02] change into their opposites when the
[02:41:04] features of the epic of transition from
[02:41:06] capitalism to a higher social and
[02:41:08] economic system had taken shape and
[02:41:10] revealed themselves in all spheres.
[02:41:12] Economically the main thing in this
[02:41:13] process is the displacement of
[02:41:15] capitalist free competition by
[02:41:17] capitalist monopoly. Free competition is
[02:41:19] the basic feature of capitalism and of
[02:41:21] commodity production generally. Monopoly
[02:41:24] is the exact opposite of free
[02:41:25] competition. But we have seen the latter
[02:41:27] being transformed into monopoly before
[02:41:29] our eyes. Creating large-scale industry
[02:41:32] and forcing out small industry,
[02:41:34] replacing large scale by still larger
[02:41:36] scale industry and carrying
[02:41:37] concentration of production and capital
[02:41:40] to the point where out of it has grown
[02:41:42] and is growing monopoly. cartels,
[02:41:44] syndicates, and trusts, and merging with
[02:41:46] them, the capital of a dozen or so
[02:41:48] banks, which manipulate billions. At the
[02:41:50] same time, the monopolies, which have
[02:41:52] grown out of free competition, do not
[02:41:54] eliminate the latter, but exist above it
[02:41:56] and alongside it, and thereby give rise
[02:41:58] to a number of very acute, intense
[02:42:00] antagonisms, frictions, and conflicts.
[02:42:02] Monopoly is the transition from
[02:42:04] capitalism to a higher system. If it
[02:42:06] were necessary to give the briefest
[02:42:08] possible definition of imperialism, we
[02:42:10] should have to say that imperialism is
[02:42:12] the monopoly stage of capitalism, such a
[02:42:14] definition would include what is most
[02:42:16] important. For on the one hand, finance
[02:42:18] capital is the bank capital of a very
[02:42:20] few big monopolist banks merged with the
[02:42:22] capital of the monopolist associations
[02:42:24] of industrialists. And on the other
[02:42:26] hand, the division of the world is the
[02:42:28] transition from a colonial policy which
[02:42:30] has extended without hindrance to
[02:42:32] territories unseased by any capitalist
[02:42:34] power to a colonial policy of monopolist
[02:42:37] possession of the territory of the world
[02:42:38] which has been completely divided up.
[02:42:40] But very brief definitions, although
[02:42:42] convenient for they sum up the main
[02:42:44] points, are nevertheless inadequate
[02:42:46] since we have to deduce from them some
[02:42:47] especially important features of the
[02:42:49] phenomenon that has to be defined. And
[02:42:51] so without forgetting the conditional
[02:42:53] and relative value of all definitions in
[02:42:55] general, which can never embrace all the
[02:42:57] concatenations of a phenomenon in its
[02:42:59] full development, we must give a
[02:43:01] definition of imperialism that will
[02:43:02] include the following five of its basic
[02:43:05] features. One, the concentration of
[02:43:07] production and capital has developed to
[02:43:09] such a high stage that it has created
[02:43:11] monopolies which play decisive role in
[02:43:14] economic life. Two, the merging of bank
[02:43:16] capital with industrial capital and the

[02:43:23] the export of capital as distinguished

[02:43:31] capitalist associations which share the
[02:43:33] world among themselves. And five, the
[02:43:35] territorial division of the whole world
[02:43:37] among the biggest capitalist powers is
[02:43:39] completed. Imperialism is capitalism at
[02:43:42] that stage of development at which the
[02:43:43] dominance of monopolies and finance
[02:43:45] capital is established. In which the
[02:43:47] export of capital has acquired
[02:43:49] pronounced importance in which the
